1207. ELLEN ROYAL and CATHERINE ROACH , feloniously breaking and entering the dwelling-house of Henry Hodges, and stealing one loaf of bread, 10 cabbages, 2 ozs. weight of coffee, and 2 1/2 lb. weight of sugar, value 9 d.; his property.
MR. W. J. PAYNE conducted the Prosecution.
HENRY HODGES . I am an umbrella-maker, and live at 1, Webb's-place, Union-street, Southwark. On Sunday, 11th May, about 10 minutes past six o'clock, I heard a noise, got up and opened my window, and saw Royal standing outside the window in the court with a young man—I asked what they wanted, and at that moment Roach stepped out of the parlour window into the court—I had bolted that window when I went to bed, about half-past eleven the night before, and the shutters were bolted—I slipped on my trowsers, ran down-stairs, and followed them—the young man dropped a loaf at the top of the court—I did not meet a policeman till I got into Mint-street; I then gave Royal into custody—they had all three gone away together—I am certain the prisoners are the parties, for they looked up at me when I was at the window—the loaf was mine, and I also missed the other things stated—they had been just inside the window.
JOHN DELANY ( policeman, M 18). I took Royal on 12th May—she was remanded, to give an opportunity of getting the other prisoner, but in consequence of not being able to get her, Royal was discharged, and the Magistrate ordered her to be re-apprehended when Roach was taken—On 21 st May I
took Roach, took her before the Magistrate, and then by his order I reapprehended Royal—she said she had none of the things, and Roach also denied it.
ROYAL — GUILTY * Aged 20.
ROACH — GUILTY .* Aged 19.
Confined Six Months.
Before Mr. Baron Rolfe.
[trialAccount t18500610-1208]
1208. JAMES M'NAIR , stealing 25 cigars and 4 oz. weight of tobacco, value 4 s. 2 d.; 8 half-crowns, 60 pence, and 120 halfpence; the property of John Hippisley, in his dwelling-house, and afterwards burglariously breaking out of the said dwelling-house.
MR. BIRNIE conducted the Prosecution.
ROBERT RATLEY . I was formerly in the V. division of police. About three o'clock in the morning of 18th April, I was on duty in Southampton-street, Lambeth, and heard some one walking in the yard at the back of the Southampton Arms public-house—I pulled myself up to the top of a side gate, looked over, and saw the prisoner coming down the stone-steps leading from the house into the yard—I could see him plainly; I have no doubt about him—I have known him for years, he is the son of the person who used to keep the house—I got down from the gate and went back for three or four yards, and he came and put his hands on the top of the gate, and his feet on the top of the bar, and got up—I asked what he did there—he said he was waiting for some Norman cows that were coming up by the railroad—I told him he had no business there, and he instantly went back—I went and spoke to Howard, another constable, and in two or three minutes I went in by another gate, leaving Howard there—I found the door at the top of the steps where I had seen the prisoner, wide open—I went in and called up the landlord, and we searched the place all over, but could not find the prisoner.
Prisoner. Q. You say you saw me quite plain? A. Yes, I was about three yards from you—I have known you three or four years, and am certain you are the man—I should know you from a thousand—I did not see you that night at the corner of Nine-Elms-lane, nor did you then tell me about the cows.
JOHN HIPPISLEY . I keep the Southampton Arms, Lambeth; I have had the house twelve months; I succeeded the prisoner's father when he left the house. On 18th April, about three o'clock in the morning, I was called up by the policeman, and found the front and back-doors open—(I had gone to bed about half-past eleven overnight, and left all the doors bolted)—I missed two papers containing copper money, eight half-crowns, and some cigars and tobacco—I had left them in a drawer—I know the prisoner quite well—I saw him in our house about seven o'clock the previous evening, the 17th—I have here a paper similar to that in which the tobacco I lost was wrapped.
DAVID PEGRAM ( policeman, L 161). On the night of 18th April the prisoner was brought to the station on a stretcher, from the effects of swallowing a screw of tobacco—he was brought in for dead, insensible—I sent for a medical man, who recovered him, and he was taken home—after he was gone, in consequence of information, I went after him, and found him at 55, Mount-street, Westminster-road, a night-house—I told him I had received information from the B division station that he was wanted, for a robbery at the Southampton Arms—he made use of some words to his companion, which I could not understand, owing to the noise in the house—I searched the prisoner, and found on him two half-crowns, two sixpences, 5 1/2 d. in copper, two screws of tobacco, and one paper which had had tobacco in—I have also produced a paper which was given me by Mr. Hippisley, and three others which I got as samples from the prosecutor's house.
JOHN HIPPISLEY re-examined. This is the paper I gave the policeman—it was cut from a memorandum-book, and the papers left behind in the drawer correspond in the figures and writing with those found on the prisoner—I do not make up the screws myself; we buy them ready made up—I sold some of these the same morning—I had in two pounds of them the same evening, at seven o'clock.
JOHN CROUCH . I am clerk to a gentleman, and live at 9, Bond-street, Vauxhall. On Wednesday, 17th April, I was at the bar of the Southampton Arms, between eleven and twelve o'clock, and saw the prisoner come in—I knew him before—I left as near twelve o'clock as possible, and I believe I left him there.
JAKES SKUDDER . I am potman to Mr. Hippisley. On the night of 17th April I shut up the shutters, about twenty-five minutes to twelve o'clock—there was then only one person in the room, and that was a porter—I saw the prisoner sitting outside the front-door on a seat—I am sure it was him—I spoke to him—I had seen him in the tap-room, between eleven and twelve—I bolted the back-door before I went to bed, about five minutes past twe e—my mistress and the servant were the last persons up—they are not here—a gentleman who was in the tap-room that night said that he expected some Alderney cows up next morning, and the prisoner was there at the time.
ANN SIMPSON . I am single, and live at 2, Vale-place, Duke-street, Westminster-road. On the morning of 18th April, I met the prisoner at Hart's public-house, in Mount-street—we had something to drink, and he went home with me—he gave me half-a-crown and sixpence, and he also gave me a cigar or a cheroot—he had given one to some females at the public-house—he paid 1 s. 11 1/2 d. for his breakfast—he had about half-a-dozen cigars in his hand—I saw no papers of tobacco.
HENRY HART . I am barman to my father, who keeps the New Crown and Cushion, in Westminster-road. On 18th April, between four and five in the morning, the prisoner came and had half-a-pint of gin, which I believe he paid for in silver—he then asked if I would oblige him by giving him five shillings for a five shilling packet of halfpence, but I said I did not want them—he then broke open a package of halfpence, and spent three or four shillings' worth treating a lot of persons at the bar, and he smoked some cheroots or cigars which he took out of his pocket—he left the house a little after six with Simpson—I did not hear of his swallowing any tobacco.
Prisoner's Defence. I know nothing of it.
(The prisoner received a good character)
GUILTY of Stealing in the dwelling-house. Aged 22.— Recommended to mercy
by the Jury.— Confined One Year.
Before Russell Gurney, Esq.
[trialAccount t18500610-1209]
1209. JAMES HICKEY , feloniously assaulting Henry De Coster, with intent to rob him.
MR. COCKLE conducted the Prosecutions.
HENRY DE COSTER . I am a tailor, and live at 28, Great Union-street, Southwark. On Monday morning, 20th May, between one and two, I was going home—a female was with me, not my wife—the prisoner came up, and asked me for money—I had no small change, and I went into a public-house to get change, and gave him 2 d.—he then asked me to give him some beer—I gave him a pint of porter, and left the house, and I was surprised to find myself quickly followed by the prisoner, who demanded more money of me, saying that he wanted to pay for his lodging, and it would come to 6 d.—he said he had just come into town off the road, and he was very tired, and had
no other means—I told him I could not give him any more, I was but a working man myself, and I had nothing else but two half-crowns in my pocket—I told him to go about his business, or I would give him in charge—he still followed me from the Obelisk in Black friars'-road all the way round Union-street, till I got opposite my own door, when he attacked me, and struck me—I struggled with him, and called for the police, who came to my assistance, or no doubt I should have been robbed—I found my watch hanging from my pocket by the guard-chain—I was perfectly sober.
Cross-examined by MR. WOOLLETT. Q. Where had you been? A. To see a friend part of the way home, it being late—it was a female friend who had been spending the evening at my house—she resided somewhere in the Waterloo-road, I do not know where—I had left her and was returning home—I called the female who was walking with me Mrs. De Coster—I did not say she was my wife—I will not swear 1 did not—that was not the same lady I had been to see home, but another—the prisoner was sober—I could not see a constable all the way he followed me, or I should have given him in charge—I did not meet any person—I did not strike him—I laid hold of him when he laid hold of me, or I should have fallen—the female cried out for police—after he got to the station he threatened to do for me after he got his liberty.
EDWARD FISHER ( policeman, M 272). In consequence of hearing a cry I went into Great Union-street, and found the prisoner and prosecutor there—the prosecutor gave him in charge for assaulting him, and demanding money of him—I took him to the station—he said to the prosecutor, "I know where you live, and I will mark you when I come out."
(The prisoner received a good character.)
GUILTY . Aged 20.— Confined Nine Months.
Before Mr. Baron Rolfe.
[trialAccount t18500610-1210]
1210. WILLIAM GROVES , burglariously breaking and entering the dwelling-house of James Linfield, with intent to steal.
MR. PAYNE conducted the Prosecution.
ELIZABETH SARAH LINFIELD . I am the wife of James Linfield, a grocer and broom-dealer, at 54, Tiverton-street, Newington. On Wednesday morning, 22nd May, I was awoke, about a quarter or twenty minutes past three o'clock, by the noise of the shop shutters breaking—I looked out of the bedroom window and saw a hat go in at the shop window—I got a light, and in about five minutes went down-stairs into the shop—I found the window and shutters open, which I had left safe about eleven over night, and saw the prisoner lying down behind the counter—I said, "Man, what do you do here?"—he made no answer, and never moved—he was very tipsy indeed—I called my husband, who got a policeman, and gave him into custody.
Cross-examined by MR. BIRNIE. Q. Did you miss anything? A. No; I had 30 s. in the till—not a thing was disturbed—I put my foot against him when I asked what he did there—he was led to the station by my husband and a policeman—he seemed to have no legs to walk upon—I had never seen him before—the shutters were very old and rotten, the least touch might have broken them.
THOMAS GRANVILLE ( policeman, M 251). I was called, and received the prisoner in charge—he was very tipsy—I could make nothing of him—he was not able to walk very well.
(The prisoner received a good character.)
NOT GUILTY .
Before Mr. Common Serjeant.
[trialAccount t18500610-1211]
1211. EDWIN SMITH , stealing 9 bagatelle-balls, value 2 l.; the goods of Benjamin Moore; having been before convicted.
MR. HUDDLESTON conducted the Prosecution.
BENJAMIN MOORE . I keep the Rose and Thistle, in Londonderry-road, Walworth—I keep a bagatelle-table in my house. On 8th April several men came to my house, I cannot tell how many—I remember two men coming, about a quarter-past eight o'clock; they went into the parlour, where the bagatelle-table is, and staid about ten minutes—when they left I went in, and the lights were turned out—as soon as I lighted them I found the nine bagatelle-balls were gone—they were worth about 2 l.—I am not able to identify either of the men—one had left a basket with Mrs. Moore in the bar, and he took it from her when they came out—I saw the man leave it—I cannot swear to his features, I did not see his face.
Prisoner. Q. I think you stated that you a had a subscription skittle-ground? A. I have—there were three or four persons playing in that ground that evening—mine is a spirit-house, a general house of business—this was on a Monday—my attention was called to that day by' my having paid my brewer—I went out after the loss to give information at the station-house—I saw the policeman Grant next day—I usually attend to the persons who play at skittles—1 attended to the bar that evening—I was not sitting in my parlour at the back of my bar, when the two men came in; I was standing in the bar, in a corner that projects into the passage—my parlour-door is down the passage—I could not have seen that parlour if I had been in my back-parlour—after the first examination, the policeman Grant called on me; the next day, I believe; he has frequently been to me—I have not treated him, he always paid for what he had—I did not undertake to describe you at the police-station, no furthar than your hat; it was a peculiar hat—I was present when my servant gave a description of the person to the police—I heard her describe you—I was in the bar, and saw two men—I will not swear to your having been in the house.
MR. HUDDLESTON. Q. What hat had he on? A. One of those Paris hats, with no beaver on it, or that shuts up—I will not swear that it did shut up.
JANE ELIZABETH PEARSON . I am servant to Mr. Moore. On the night of 8th April I saw the prisoner at my master's house; I saw him when he was going out; he came from the parlour where the bagatelle-table is, along to the bar—he asked Mrs. Moore for a basket he had left, and she gave it shim—I am sure the prisoner is the man—there was a person came out of the parlour at the time the prisoner did; 1 do not know that he was with him—it was twenty-five minutes past eight o'clock.
Prisoner. Q. Where were you standing? A. At the bar—my master was inside the bar—it was a long flag basket, a sort of tool-basket—when I first saw you at the station I had not a doubt of your being the person from the alteration of the dress—the man had on a light plaid coat, with dark stripes—when the policeman came to fetch me I did not have any conversation with him and Mr. Moore; I was behind them—I do not recollect that I heard any conversation with them—my master has a subscription skittle-ground; he generally attends to it—there were two or three persons there—those who left the parlour went in the skittle-ground—I attend to the parlour sometimes—I took a pot of porter to those men at eight o'clock—a few minutes after I took the beer in, the men came out, and went to play—I did not see any
strange man in the parlour; there were none but what I knew—the man who took the basket came from the passage from the direction of the parlour—I do not know what that man had had—you were not in the parlour when I took in the beer—there was not much playing in the bagatelle-room after twenty-five minutes past eight that evening—I remember the first examination at the police-court—I cannot tell who described you to Mrs. Moore on our return home—I dare say there was some conversation; I cannot recollect what it was—I did not describe you to Mrs. Moore—I do not know that Mr. Moore did—I do not recollect how soon afterwards I saw the policeman Grant—he did not advise how I should give evidence—I cannot tell whether Mr. and Mrs. Moore were present when I gave a description of the man to the policeman—I gave a description before the policeman came—I do not know that Mr. Moore asked me the description before he went to the station—we were all in a muddle—the last person I saw come from the parlour to the bar was the person who took the basket—there were two men came out—I will not swear whether any one else went into the parlour at that time—I do not know whether the skittle-ground was searched by Mr. Moore—he was not there when the men came out of the parlour, he was in the bar—I described one person to the policeman, in the hearing of Mr. and Mrs. Moore—I was here last Session—Grant did not say to me, "Mind you swear as you did before, or you will get yourself in a mess"—there was no witness near me and Grant, who overheard that—Grant did not say, "There is a witness for Smith; let us go further"—it was my master who first discovered the loss.
MR. HUDDLESTON. Q. Did you hear the prisoner apply last Session to have the case postponed till this Session because be had no witnesses here? A. Yes; I had no conversation of the kind the prisoner has mentioned—when I saw him I knew him again instantly—I recognized him of my own accord—I have no doubt at all he is the man—as soon as the loss was discovered I gave a description of the man who had come out for the basket—when I saw him at the station he was dressed differently, but in spite of that I recognized him by his features—I said, "You are the man, although you have got a different coat on"—he said, "I don't know where your house is"—the prisoner was not in the parlour when I took the pot of beer in—it was for a young man who is a customer—I did not see the prisoner go in, but I saw him come out.
COURT. Q. You know those persons that were in the parlour when you went in; did you see what they were doing? A. They were playing at bagatelle—the man who called for the basket, came from the passage that leads to the parlour, right past the bar—there is no other way of getting to the passage than coming from the parlour.
MARGARET MOORE . I am the wife of Benjamin Moore. I was in the bar on the evening of 8th April; I saw the prisoner there soon after eight o'ctock—he came straight into the house, and a young man with him—he brought a basket with him, and asked me to take charge of it—he went into the parlour with the young man—I saw the door shut after they went into the parlour—I had heard the bagatelle balls about five minutes before that—the lights were then burning, and as the prisoner went into the parlour, I saw the lights there—no other person went into the parlour or came out after the prisoner went in—the prisoner and the young man did not call for any refreshment—they came out in ten minutes or a quarter of an hour—the prisoner asked for the basket—the young man did not speak—I said to the prisoner, "I suppose these are flower-roots in the basket"—he said, "No, they are rhubarb
roots"—my husband was in the bar at the time—I went to the parlour some time after—I found the gas was out, and the balls were gone—no other persons had gone into the parlour but those persons—soon afterwards some persons came and wished to go in, but the lights were out, and the balls were gone.
Prisoner. Q. You were not sitting in the bar-parlour when the man came in? A. No; I was standingin the bar—my husband generally attends to the skittle-ground, but he was in the bar when the men came in—I do not know whether he saw you, he might have been engaged—I saw the man, he had a plaid coat on, rather short—I and the servant described the man to the police the next morning—I saw the policeman, Grant, on the morning after the examination—I had not seen you then at the police-station—the policeman asked how you were dressed, and whether you had a light complexion; and as far as I remembered I told him—I saw Mrs. Graham at the police-station; I had some conversation with her; I did not describe you to her—when Grant came to oar house there was not a general conversation in order to learn how the evidence should be given—he did not say, "Stick to that evidence"—he was not treated by me—what my husband did I cannot say—the basket was a long basket, such as carpenters would carry—I should think there were about six persons in the parlour when the pot of beer was ordered—some of them left to go to the skittle-ground—my husband attends to that ground, but he was in the bar; I do not know whether he was serving—he did not leave to go to attend the men in the skittle-ground—it was not subscription-night—the two men came in together, one carrying the basket—the one that left the basket bad a check coat on—I swear that was you—we have only one parlour—I was in the bar the whole evening—I can swear those men came from the parlour—I am sure they did not come from the skittle-ground.
MR. HUDDLESTON. Q. Who is Mrs. Graham? A. The wife of Mr. Graham, who lost some balls—that is another charge against the prisoner—when I went to identify the prisoner, there were two others called with him—they asked me, "Do you know either of these persons?"—I said, "Yes," and pointed the prisoner out.
JAMES GRANT ( police-sergeant, P 11). From information, I took the prisoner into custody—I charged him with stealing the bagatelle-balls from Mr. Moore's—he said he did not, he did not know where the house was, and he had never been in the house.
Prisoner. Q. From whom did you receive the information of the robbery? A. At the station—I went to Mr. Moore's house—Mrs. Moore and the servant gave me a description of you—I had seen you repeatedly before—I knew where you lived—I made some inquiries—there were certain reasons for my not going to the house—I was not certain that you were living there—I knew you went there occasionally, and you were to be found there at different times—I saw you before you went to the house, and followed you into the house—you gave me the address in South-street—the address was in your pocket—I have never seen you in company with thieves, you were generally alone when I saw you—as soon as they gave me the description, I said I believed I knew the person—the servant came to the station, and identified you at once; and Mrs. Moore, as soon as ever she saw you with three or four others, pointed you out—two persons were described to me, but you were the most likely person—I did not find a basket or any check coat at your lodgings—I have been to Mr. Moore's several times since that—I had some conversation with them—I was not treated—I paid for what I had.
MR. HUDDLESTON. Q. Did the description given you by Mrs. Moore and her servant answer the description of the prisoner? A. Yes.
Prisoner. Q. You had no difficulty in finding me? A. I looked for you, but I was not watching the house night and day—I had some difficulty in finding you out, or I should have taken you before—you were in the habit of going out at all hours—I have not seen you late at night, but I have heard that you came in very early in the morning—I did not see you after the robbery—you were pointed out to me by a constable in the Walworth-road, about nine months ago, as a convicted thief, and since then I have frequently seen you; and as soon as I received this information, I looked for you—I had seen you at No. 5, George-street. Walworth-common—I knew afterwards that you did not live there, but I did not know at the time—I made inquiries from 9th April till the 22nd, and looked for you as well—I took you on the 22nd.
MR. HUDDLESTON. Q. You say he was pointed out? A. Yes, as a convicted thief—I kept my eye on him, and if I saw any constable, I pointed him out to him—after I had received this information, I looked out for him, and took him the first time I saw him.
MRS. MOORE re-examined. I saw the person who left the basket go into the parlour, and another person with him—at that time there was no other person in the parlour.
Prisoner's Defence. It is a circumstance of identity; after the witnesses I shall call, you will satisfy your mind that I am not the person who is guilty: I do not know the house.
Witnesses for the Defence.
ANN MURRAY . I have known the prisoner a year and a half—I do not remember 8th April—he was given into custody on the 22nd—he never wore any other coat but the one he has on, except a black one—I had never seen any acquaintance of his coming to my place, wearing a check-coat—I cannot tell what time he was home on 8th April—he never had a check-coat on—I saw that he possessed a carpenter's basket—when he was given into custody, I was surprised at my place being searched—I take in washing—he might have taken things in my place if he had been disposed.
Cross-examined by MR. HUDDLESTON. Q. You have known him a year and a-half? A. Yes, and his wife nearly two years—he had an opportunity to take things from me—I do not come here to give him a character—I know he has been convicted—he was in prison when Mrs. Smith came to my house—he lodges at my house—I do not know what he does—since he has been at my place he has never done work—he is something of a carpenter or a cabinet-maker—I do not know where he works—I have been in his room, it is always open—he has gone out at eight or nine o'clock in the morning—he has come home at ten or eleven at night—he sleeps at the top of the house.
WILLIAM GREEN . I did live at No. 5, George-street. On 8th April the prisoner came in, between five and six o'clock in the evening—he stopped there till after the public-houses were shut up between twelve and one; he played a number of games of cribbage with me and Barker—he did not pay his reckoning, because the beer-house was shut up.
Cross-examined. Q. Barker was playing. A. Yes; I am a coster-monger—I get my living by selling fish and other things in the street—I sold fish the week before last, all that week, round Peckham. I have a two-wheeled barrow—I had only one day's work last week—that was on Saturday, selling gooseberries and rhubarb—I cannot tell what I was doing last Tuesday fortnight—I was not asked to come here as a witness, I was subpoenaed—here is my subpoena—this was the first intimation that I had that I was to be called as a witness—nobody had been talking with me about what we had been
doing that evening till I got this subpoena—some young man brought it this morning—on 6th April I was up in the Walworth-road selling rhubarb, and cabbages, and greens; Barker was with me—I think that day I dined at Edward Smith's, who lives up a turning in South-street—I then lived at 5, George-street—I now live at my mother's—I had seen the prisoner before the night of 8th April, and spoken to him—he did not live in that house—Barker now lives along with me at my mother's in Waterloo-street—he did lodge with me in George-street—on the night we were playing cribbage there was me and Barker, the prisoner, Edward Smith and his wife—no one but us five—I had been with Barker all day—we had been to market, and could not buy anything—we came and staid at home, and in the evening we played at cribbage—Barker played first, and then I did—it was two-handed cribbage—we only played for beer—a pint of beer a game—we had two pots of beer brought in—Edward Smith brought them both in—Edward Smith did not play—he did not bet—Mrs. Smith did not play—I think I played three games—I won them all—I think the prisoner played more games with Barker, but I eannot say how many, for I was out a little while—I went out between eight and nine, that was before I played—I had seen Barker play, but I cannot tell how many games—I was in again in half an hour's time—Barker did not play after I played—we played and talked between the games—we had a cribbage-board—I cannot tell how long it took to play the three or four games—I have never been in trouble, nor before a Magistrate—I do not know John George Sharpe, nor Harriet Rathbone—I have heard Mr. Smith talk about them—I never saw them.
WILLIAM BARKER . I remember playing cribbage on Monday, 8th April, a fortnight before the prisoner was taken—in consequence of our not having money to buy anything we stopped at home and smoked our pipes, and drank tome beer—I played first, and then Green played three or four games, and then I played again—the prisoner came between five and six o'clock, and left between one and two in the morning.
Cross-examined. Q. What are you? A. A hawker—I hawk all manner of things, fruit and fish—I live with Green, in Waterloo-street—I hawked yesterday—Green did not hawk with me, but he was out selling cocoanuts—the day before yesterday he was round with his barrow in his usual business—he works every day—I know all about him—he was not out all the week because he could not buy anything, but he was out on Saturday with gooseberries and rhubarb—I should say he took 11 s. or 12 s.; he earns something every day when he is out—I hardly remember where I was on 6th April, I think I was out—the first time I was asked to come here was on Saturday last—Mrs. Smith asked me to come and speak what I knew of the day he was playing cards with me—she asked me what day that was, and I told her—Green did not go out at all during the time we were playing—I am sure of that, as far as I can recollect—I believe I played first—I think Green was rather the luckiest—I think we had between two and three pots of beer—I think it was three—Mr. Smith that I lodge with fetched the beer—I think, on 4th April, I was out round the streets with my barrow selling fish—I have never been in trouble in my life.
Prisoner. Q. I think if you charge your memory you will find that Green went out? A. I do not remember it—I will swear positively that you did not leave the house till twelve o'clock at night.
JAMES GRANT re-examined. The public-house is about a mile and a half from where I apprehended the prisoner—I know 5, George-street—that is about a mile and a half from Moore's.
GEORGE QUINNEAR ( police-sergeant, P I). I produce a certificate of the prisoner's former conviction, at Newington—( read—Convicted, July, 1848, by the name of Thomas Smith, and confined six months)—the prisoner is the person.
GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18500610-1212]
1212. EDWIN SMITH was again indicted for stealing 9 bagatelle-balls, value 30 s.; the goods of Robert Graham.
ROBERT GRAHAM . I keep the Rose and Crown beer-house, in William-place, Walworth-road. On 2nd Feb. I had nine bagatelle-balls, and I lost them between a quarter-past nine and ten o'clock, as near as possible—I saw the prisoner that night at my house—he came up-stairs into the room, and requested to play bagatelle—I did not know him before—I am quite sure he is the person—he came with a person named White, who has been convicted—I knew White before—they played up-stairs, in the club-room—there was no other person there—I saw them leave both together, and I had my suspicions—I sent my wife up-stairs the next moment—she called out, and I went out after the men—I ran them down a street, and they escaped into the gardens—they ran, and got away—White was afterwards taken—I am quite sure the prisoner was one of the two.
Prisoner. Q. You say two persons came into your house? A. Yes; there were more persons down-stairs, but only two in the room up-stairs, you and your companion—I gave information to the police, and gave a description of the two parties—I went to Horsemonger-lane to prosecute White—I know Rawlings—my testimony and my wife's did not convict him and White—that was for another offence—Rawlings was not at my house on 2nd Feb.—I do not know your wife—she might have called at my house—since you have been taken on this case, no one has called to ask me to give them information respecting White and Rawlings—your father called on me one night—I did not swear that White and Rawlings were in my house—you were dressed in a check coat.
SOPHIA GRAHAM . I am the wife of Robert Graham. On 2nd Feb., about a quarter or half-past nine o'clock at night, two persons came into our house—I am quite sure the prisoner is one of them—they asked if any one was up-stairs—there was no one but my husband—they went up, and asked my husband for a pint of beer and two pipes—my husband left them in the room, playing at the bagatelle board, which he gave them—I suppose they had asked for it—I had seen it before, and it was quite safe—I afterwards saw the two men come down-stairs—my husband was not in the room some part of the time—when the men left he was in the bar—when they came down the other man paid for what they had had—the prisoner waited for him, and they went out together—after they had left, my husband told me to run up-stairs—I went up, and the balls were gone—I am quite sure the prisoner is the man.
Prisoner. Q. Were you a witness against White? A. Yes; Rawlings was tried with him—Rawlings was sentenced to four months, and White to twelve months—I and my husband were not the only witnesses—we did not swear that those two men were the only men in our room that night—we did not swear to Rawlings—White was the person that came in with you—Rawlings was not convicted on our case, but on Mr. Gardener's case—White and Rawlings were convicted for Mr. Gardener's case.
JAMES GRANT ( police-sergeant, P 11). I took the prisoner on 22nd of April—I told him he was charged with being concerned with another in stealing bagatelle balls from the Rose and Crown beer-shop, on 2nd Feb.—he said he did not know where the house was, and never was in it.
Prisoner's Defence. I have endeavoured to ascertain who this White is, to clear myself, and I was obliged to write to the Magistrate; word came to me that White and Rawlings bad been convicted on the same charge; I feel convinced that Quinnear and Grant have got up the case against me.
GUILTY . Aged 32.— Confined Twelve Months.
[trialAccount t18500610-1213]
1213. JOHN BAXTER , stealing 1 brass tap, value 3 s. 6 d.; the goods of John Beard; having been before convicted.
DANIEL HALEY ( policeman, M 181). On 24th May, I was on duty in Kent-street, at a little before three o'clock in the morning—I stopped the prisoner coming out of Hunter-street, and asked what be had in his pockets—he said a pint pot which he found near the Borough-road—I searched his pocket, and found in it three brass taps—this is one—he said he bought them of a man in Blackfriars-road for a shilling—I took him to the station, and went to Mr. Beard's, and showed him this tap—it had been in the yard at the back of his house—I observed some footmarks—I took the prisoner's shoe, and put it into the marks, and it corresponded—I saw the impression before I put the shoe in it.
JOHN BEARD . I am a gas lighter—this tap is mine—I saw it safe in the water-butt in my yard on the night before, about nine o'clock, and missed it about four next morning.
Prisoner. I bought the three taps of a man in the Blackfriars-road.
WILLIAM WEAVERS . I produce a certificate of the prisoner's conviction at Newington—( read—Convicted March, 1850, and confined two months)—I was then a constable, and was present—he is the person.
GUILTY . Aged 20.— Confined Twelve Months.
(There were two other indictments against the prisoner.)
[trialAccount t18500610-1214]
1214. JOHN WHITE, HENRY PAULTON , and GEORGE WIXCEY , stealing 1 purse, value 6 d.; and 1 half-crown, 1 shilling, and 1 farthing; the property of Emily Amelia Hunt: Wixcey having been before convicted.
EMILY AMELIA HUNT . I am tingle; I live in Manner's-street, Blackfriars-road. On 19th April, I went into a coffee-house in Waterloo-road, about two o'clock in the moruing—I had a cup of coffee—I sat down by the side of White—I had not known him before—in ten minutes or a quarter of an hour he got up and left the house—I missed my purse, and my pocket was cut—I had had in my purse a half-crown, a shilling, and a farthing—I had it safe when I went into the coffee-house, and paid for the coffee out of it—I spoke to the people of the coffee-house, and then went to the police—I have not got my money or purse since.
Cross-examined by MR. RIBTON. Q. What are you? A. I have been in service, but I have been very ill the last month or six weeks, and not able to go—I had been to the theatre, and had had a glass of ale—the theatre was over at twelve o'clock—we had a walk—I think it was a little before two when I got to the coffee-house—I was not there longer than half an hour—I had no conversation with White, nor he with me—there were a great many people there; I spoke to none of them—I was not asleep; I was half-dozing—my eyes were shut.
GEORGE SAMUEL DURRANT . I was in the coffee-shop where the protecutrix was—the three prisoners were there—Wixcey was sitting in a box on the other side—White changed places with a man, and sat close to the prosecutrix—Paulton was standing by the fire-place—while the prosecutrix was sitting by White, I saw him lift up her clothes—he said to Wixcey be wanted a chive,
and Wixcey gave him a knife—Whit then lifted up her clothes again, cut a hole in her pocket, and took a little bead purse out—he then got up and walked out—the other two prisoners walked after him—I went out after they left, and saw Wixcey standing about with his basket of oysters.
Cross-examined. Q. What are you? A. A potter; I had been to the theatre, and was too late to get in at home—I went to the coffee-house next door to the theatre—I had a cup of coffee—I went to sleep; I cannot say for how long—I went in about one o'clock—perhaps it was about two when I went to sleep—I slept till about four—White was not talking to the prosecutrix; he sat by her—that was about a quarter before four—I was sitting right opposite—she was asleep—I did not say anything.
CHARLES BURGESS GOFF ( police-sergeant, L 8). I went to Charles-street, Waterloo-road, and took White and Paulton in charge.
HENRY HOUOHAM ( policeman, L 159.) I produce a certificate of Wixcey's conviction at Newington—read—( Convicted March, 1846, and confined twenty-one days)—he is the person.
WHITE — GUILTY .† Aged 28.— Transported for Seven Years.
PAULTON — NOT GUILTY .
WIXCEY — GUILTY . Aged 20.— Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18500610-1215]
1215. ELLEN GRANT , stealing 1 half-crown; he moneys of Hugh Rowen, from his person: having been before convicted.
HUGH ROWEN . I am a mason's labourer. On 12th May, between five and six o'clock in the morning I went into a whiskey-shop in St. Jame's-place, Graham-lane—the prisoner was there—she asked me if I was not going to give her a glass—I said, "No"—she put her hand in my waistcoat-pocket, and took out a half-crown and two penny-pieces—I shook her hand; the half-crown fell, and another female picked it up—I gave the prisoner in charge.
CHARLES JACKSON LEWIS ( policeman, M 76). I was sent for, and took the prisoner—I told her the charge—she said she knew nothing about it, and it was a b—y shame she should be taken when he was talking to another girl.
Prisoner's Defence. I am quite innocent; I had never seen the man before.
JOHN DELANY ( police-sergeant, M 18). I produce a certificate of the prisoner's conviction at Newington— read —( Convicted May, 1849, and confined three months)—she is the person.
GUILTY . Aged 25.— Confined Twelve Months.
[trialAccount t18500610-1216]
1216. SPENCER BENHAM , stealing 1 shirt-collar, value 1 s. 6 d., and 1 sixpence; the property of James Rogers, his master: to which he pleaded
GUILTY . Aged 18.— Confined Two Months.
[trialAccount t18500610-1217]
1217. HENRY FORD , stealing 91 feet of pipe, and two metal taps, value 35 s.; the goods of John Stevens and another, his masters.
WILLIAM HENRY WILLIAMS . I am in the employ of John and James Stevens, in Bridge-road, Southwark. The prisoner was in their employ—he left rather better than twelve monthsago, directly after this happened—it was his duty to execute any orders he received, to take the materials and book them to the number that corresponded with the order—to take them from the warehouseman—he was to do any job that he was ordered to do—the account was sent in for this work—it was not paid.
Cross-examined by MR. CHARNOCK. Q. How long was he in the service? A. Two or three times; perhaps, twelve months altogether—he was a gasfitter
—the orders are generally given to the warehouseman—I do not gene-rally go to see the work done; I go sometimes, if there is anything worth notice—a man named Rainer was in our employ, as a labourer—I believe when a labourer was out at work, if there was a material wanted, he came and fetched it—the foreman had the charge of hiring any labourer—when the men or the labourer bring back any goods, the warehouseman, Mr. Baker, takes them in—perhaps at that time we had thirty or forty men—the prisoner left of his own accord—we seat a policeman after him—a reward has been offered for him for twelve months—I do not keep the books; I sometimes take the money—I superintend the whole concern—I have seen the prisoner drunk.
JOSEPH BAKER . I am warehouseman in the employ of Messrs, Stevens; it is my duty to give out to the gas-fitters anything required for the job they are employed in. I recollect the prisoner being in their service—I gave him some tin pipe twelve months ago—he told me it was to do work at the British and Foreign School—about twenty-four feet of pipe was delivered to him on 20th and 21st Feb., 1849—I have not seen any of those articles since—I have seen a bill which refers to them—I did not hear anybody speak to him as to what had become of those goods, but I know he was sent back to point out where the goods were.
Cross-examined. Q. Do you keep a book? A. Yes; I have the charge of the warehouse, and enter everything that goes out and that comes back—I do not make mistakes—I have not had accounts constantly sent back with mistakes—I had one sent back from Mr. Morley's, in Wood-street, but it was not my error, but that of the person who measured the work—I am hardly ever absent when the men bring the work back—every inch of pipe that is brought back is put down—I have not frequently, when things have been brought back, omitted to put them down—these things were taken partly by Rainer, and partly by the prisoner—Rainer is here—they were never brought back—the men bring back what they do not use from various jobs, and they are taken off the bill—I have the bill here and the day-book—Mr. Williams receives money, and Mr. Stevens, and Mr. Scales—Mr. Scales is not here—this is the book in which I entered the things I supplied the prisoner with—here is the place where he asked me for materials to work at the British and Foreign School—it was on 20th Feb., 1849—that was the first day we supplied him with anything for that job—he had 12 ft. 5 in. of 18 in. pipe.
Cross-examined. Q. Here seems to be an erasure here; you make mistakes in your books? A. No; this word double is written on an erasure—I cannot see an erasure where this 12 is—here is an erasure—the whole of this is on an erasure—these alterations were made afterwards.
COURT. Q. What else is entered beside the pipe? A. One 3/4 in. union, one 3/4 T, four screws, and other things—I heard the prisoner say at the Court that he got drunk and lost it—this paper is the writing of Mr. Taylor, our clerk—I do not see that there are any screws in it—the next article is half-inch union-pipe—here is twelve inches in one of these books, and twelve in the other—one of these is the Tuesday's-book, and while the clerk is posting one, I use the other—I do not copy from one book into the other—this is the Tuesday's book; things go from hence into the journal—Wednesday's work is put in this other book—I swear 1 delivered these things myself to the prisoner or his labourer; I will not swear to which—I cannot tell how much I delivered to the prisoner.
NOT GUILTY .
(There was another indictment against the prisoner, on which no evidence Was offered.)
[trialAccount t18500610-1218]
1218. THOMAS WARD and JOHN SKELTON , stealing 4 bushels of oats, value 10 s.; the goods of John Edwards Bennett, their master; and WILLIAM RAYMENT , for feloniously receiving the same.
MR. PARRY conducted the Prosecution.
JOHN EDWARDS BENNETT . I am a hay and corn salesman, at King's Arms Wharf, Belvedere-road, Lambeth—I contract to supply hay and corn to the Government—Ward has been in my employ for eight or ten years as carman, and Skelton from twelve to fifteen years—he was granary-man—it was the carman's duty to draw oats to the barracks—I have a very slight knowledge of Rayment—it was Skelton's duty, as granary-man, to load Ward's cart—Ward takes out, in the course of a day, twenty or thirty quarters—I was in the habit of supplying the Regent's-park Barracks—I sent there every morning twelve quarters of oats, in twenty-four sacks—Rayment has never bought corn of me—I was not on the 3rd April to supply him with a sack of oats—Skelton and Ward had no authority from me to supply him with a sack of oats that day—I had employed Romain and Garforth to watch my premises at six o'clock that morning.
Cross-examined by MR. HUDDLESTON? Q. Were you ever in partnership? A. Yes; that has been terminated from two to three years—I never was a bankrupt—no trustees have been concerned for me or for the benefit of my creditors—I have fourteen or fifteen carmen besides Ward—I have no other regular granary-man but Skelton—there is a man employed occasionally, if an extra man is wanted—I send out about thirty-six quarters of oats in a day, and hay and straw—no man takes corn but Ward, except on particular occasions—he always takes the corn to the barracks.
Cross-examined by MR. PARNELL. Q. Did you give this particular order to Ward that day? A. There was no particular order, because it was his regular duty—suppose another order was to be taken out the same day, it would not be his duty to take it at that time—it was his duty to go to the barracks, and no other man—if he had oats to take elsewhere the same day, it would not be his duty to take them together—he had the twenty-four sacks for the barracks, and I never allowed him to take any other—I do not pretend to speak to this sack or these oats—my foreman is not here.
WILLIAM ROMAIN ( police-sergeant, L 38). On 27th May, I watched the prosecutor's premises, from six o'clock in the morning, till twenty minutes before eight—while I was watching, I saw Skelton lowering down sacks from the granary, and Ward had a cart receiving the sacks from the granary—Garforth was with me—I saw the cart loaded, and come out from the wharf about twenty minutes before eight—1 followed the cart—it stopped at a coffee-shop a short time—Ward went in, and left the cart outside—I watched it, and never lost sight of it—Ward came out, and the cart then went over Westminster-bridge, up Parliament-street, up St. Martin's-lane, through St. Giles, and to Cheyne-mews, Bloorasbury—that is not the way to the Regent's-park Barracks—Rayment has a stable in Cheyne-mews—Ward was with the cart all the time—from the time it left the granary till it got to Rayment's, there was no sack put in it or taken out of it—when the cart got to Rayment's, it pulled up in front of the stable, and Rayment came out of the stable immediately—Ward did not knock or ring, the gates were wide open—Ward got on the load, and handed a sack from the load on to Rayment's shoulder—Rayment carried it up a ladder into the loft—I followed him up into the loft, leaving Garforth in charge of the cart and Ward—when I got into the loft, Rayment was turning round, and was about to shoot the oats into a bin—I said, "Halloo! what are you going to do with these?"—he said, "The
carman has asked me to leave them here"—I said, "You don't require to shoot them for that; bring them down again"—he brought them down, and I desired him to pitch them against the stable-door—I then asked Ward how many sacks be had in his cart—he said "Twenty-four"—I said, "Then you brought twenty-five from the wharf"—he said, "No, I did not"—he said "I know nothing about that sack," pointing to the sack that Rayment had just brought down—I said, "You must consider yourselves both in custody," and I took them both to St. Giles' station, leaving Garforth with the cart and the two horses—at the station I explained the charge to the inspector; and Rayment said, "That sack of oats did not belong to Mr. Bennett"—he said he met Ward in St. Giles' on his journey, and he asked him to leave the sack of oats as he went along—I told the inspector I was prepared to prove that was false—Rayment did not make any answer to that—I had not lost sight of the cart—nothing of that kind had taken place at St. Giles', or anywhere else—the sack had a mark on it; I have it here, and a sample of oats from the barracks, after I shot them, and a sample from the sack that Rayment had, and a sample from the bulk—I and Garforth took the oats to the barracks and shot them—there were twenty-four sacks.
Cross-examined by MR. HUDDLESTON. Q. When you went up into the loft, where was Ward? A. Down-stairs with the cart—I followed Rayment through the stable, up the ladder into the loft—I had the conversation with Rayment up-stairs—Ward did not hear what I said to Rayment in the first place, but he heard the conversation afterwards—it was about twenty minutes before eight in the morning when they left the wharf—I had been watching from six—I and Garforth did not keep together all the way, but we never lost sight of the load—we were not more than from twelve to twenty yards from it at any time—I saw nothing of Skelton after Ward had started—I did not go into any shop—Garforth was with me the whole way—he never was more than a dozen yards from me—I did not make any note of what passed at the time, or since—I saw my depositions at the police-court, and I have seen them since at Mr. Bennett's office at Furnival's-inn—I did not go there to see them—I heard them read over there—they were read, to see whether they were correct—they had been taken down before at the police-court—they were read to me there—I was asked if they were correct—I signed them—what was read at Mr. Bennett's was merely a copy—they were read to me, to see if the copy was correct—I had not the depositions—they were quite correct—I did not ask to have them read—I have not seen them since, nor a copy of them—it was not for the purpose of refreshing my memory that they were read, I did not require it.
Cross-examined by MR. PARNEIL. Q. When you were before the Magistrate you could not recollect what Rayment had said? A. Yes, I did; there is my statement of what I said—Garforth did not remind me of what the statement was—I did not turn to Garforth and ask him what Rayment had said—I had no conversation with Garforth about what Rayment had said—I only went once to Mr. Bennett's office—I was told by his brother that he was the solicitor, and if I were going that way, I should call in as I passed—I did not go by appointment—my beat is in various parts of Lambeth—I am at times a plain-clothes man—it was from five to seven days before the Sessions that I went to Mr. Bennett's—it was after I had been before the Magistrate the last time—the depositions were only once read over at Mr. Bennett's—Garforth went with me—he happened to be passing that way—we did not talk about it as we went along, to my knowledge—I will swear we did not—I never lost sight of the cart—I did not notice how many times it turned—I
took care to follow it close up—the sacks were lying down in the cart—one was not at all separated from the others—there was not one sack apart from the others.
THOMAS GARFORTH ( policeman, L 151). On 27th May, about six o'clock in the morning, I was watching Mr. Bennett's premises with Romain—about seven I saw a cart under the granary-window—I saw a sack of oats being let down—when the cart was loaded J saw it leave the wharf, and I followed it with Romain from the time it left the wharf till it got to Cheyne-mews—I did not once lose sight of it—Ward was driving it—when it got to Cheyne-mews, it drove down and stopped opposite Rayment's stable—I saw Ward get on the cart, and hand a sack of oats on to Rayment's shoulder—I have no doubt there was a word or two passed between them, but not many words—Rayment took the sack into the stable, and Romain followed him in—I took charge of Ward and the cart—I said to Ward, "You must consider yourself in my custody, for stealing a sack of oats that is gone in the stable"—he said, "Steal them, eh! I was ordered to deliver them here by my employer"—the sack of oats was brought down shortly by Rayment, and Romain followed him down—I eventually took care of the cart, and Ward and Rayment were taken by Romain—I and Romain went with the cart afterwards to the barracks, and Romain took a sample of the oats—I afterwards went to the Belvedere Wharf, and saw Skelton—the foreman asked him, in my presence, how many oats he had put on Ward's cart that morning—he said, "Twelve quarters, I think," and after hesitating a little he said, "After I had given him twelve quarters, as I thought, he said he had not enough by one sack, and I took a sack and filled it and gave it him"—I then asked him whether they used sacks with different names on them—he said, "Yes, we do use odd sacks sometimes"—I said, "Did you have a sack with the name of Anderson on it?"—he said, "We had a sack with that name"—I then took him into custody—I kept sight of the cart all the time—I did not see Rayment in St. Giles's, or anywhere else, put a sack of oats on the cart.
Cross-examined by MR. HUDDLESTON. Q. Have you refreshed your memory at all by your depositions, since they were taken? A. No; I wrote down a part of the deposition, a few words that Skelton made use of in the loft—I wrote it down, because I had so many things to think of—I often do make a memorandum—Romain is similarly employed—I do not know whether he made a copy of anything—I have not seen the depositions, nor heard them read over—nor copies of them from the time they were made—yes, I beg pardon, they were read over in my presence at Mr. Bennett's; I had forgotten that for the moment—I have not got the bit of paper here that I wrote: I left it at home—I keep a memorandum of all cases that I have—I look at it before I come out.
Cross-examined by MR. PARNELL. Q. How came you at Mr. Bennett's? A. I went with Romain—he said he was going over there, and I might as well take a walk with him—it was at the Southwark Police-court that he said that—I think it was last Friday; I will not be certain—I went before the Grand Jury yesterday—when I got to Rayment's it was about eight o'clock, or it might be ten minutes past—I did not have anything in going along—previous to the cart coming out of the yard we went and had a pint of beer—in going along with the prisoners they wished to have something—I was in the cab with them, and Romain came and asked if I would take anything—I said, "You may bring me a glass of stout," and he brought me one—I was with Romain when he was being examined—I do not recollect that he spoke to me while lie was being examined—I believe there was a mistake in
the clerk putting down a name—when he was asked what Rayment had said, he said what Ward said—I did not call his attention to it; Mr. Bennett did—he looked at me; but as they will not allow policemen to speak together he did not say anything.
MR. BENNETT re-examined. These oats are just the same as I am sending to the barracks—I have not the slightest doubt that they are the same—they are worth 10 s. a sack when delivered.
Cross-examined by MR. HUDDLESTON. Q. Are they Russian oats? A. I do not know what to call them—they are good oats—they are worth 16 s. or 16 s. 6 d. a quarter, but then there are expenses—I shall give them no other name than good oats.
( The prisoners' statements before the Magistrate were read as follows: —Ward says, "The sack of oats brought here was not the sack taken off my cart"—Skelton says, "I know nothing about his having twenty-five sacks that morning, and I did not say that Anderson's sack went away that morning; I said that a sack with the name of Duncan went away"—Rayment says, "The sack of oats did not belong to Mr. Bennett at all.")
(Ward and Rayment received good characters).
WARD — GUILTY. Aged 40.— Recommended to mercy by the Prosecutor and
Jury.— Confined Three Months. RAYMENT — GUILTY . Aged 61.—
Recommended to mercy by the Jury. — Confined Six Months.
SKELTON — NOT GUILTY .
